It takes so long that I miss prep work, and I’ve ended up late for classes because of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think something terrible would happen if you didn't check your appearance or adjust the camera angle before recording or joining a conference?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering parts of my face with my hand, and sometimes re-recording the whole thing so many times that I miss prep work and end up late with my classes.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I start thinking the students, parents, and other staff must be staring at that instead of actually listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face. Especially when I see myself on lesson videos, one side looks crooked and sort of swollen to me. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking about it, and I feel like students or parents or other staff are staring at that instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t really choose to think about it—it just comes in, especially after I see myself on those lesson videos. Once I notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen, I can’t seem to let it go, and I start feeling like everyone must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of these thoughts about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep adjusting the camera angle and replaying clips over and over. I’ll cover part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better, and sometimes I re-record the whole thing again and again. It takes so long that I miss prep work, and I’ve ended up late for classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something terrible would happen if you didn't check your appearance or adjust the camera angle before recording or joining a conference?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say something terrible exactly, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’ll post something where my face looks really off. So I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face, and sometimes I end up re-recording it over and over. It gets out of hand—I miss prep time and I’ve been late to classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of distress. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on the lesson videos. Once I see it, it’s hard to focus on anything else, and I feel like the students, parents, and staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they saw your face as it is, without you adjusting the camera angle or re-recording?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think they’d notice it right away—the crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And then I feel like they’d be focused on that instead of what I’m saying, like students or parents or staff would just be staring at it and judging me.\n\nDoctor: When you point out the crooked or swollen side of your face to others, like friends or family, do they notice it or agree with your assessment?\n\nPatient: They usually don’t really agree with me, or they’ll say they don’t see what I’m talking about. But I still can’t stop seeing it, especially on the lesson videos. Once I notice that crooked, swollen-looking side, I feel like everyone watching—students, parents, staff—must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I suppose I might be more distracted sometimes, but I can’t say it’s affected my relationships in a definite way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your concerns about your face are unreasonable or irrational?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it I feel like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Especially when I see myself on lesson videos, one side looks crooked and sort of swollen to me. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking about it, and I feel like students or parents or other staff are staring at that instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t really choose to think about it—it just comes in, especially after I see myself on those lesson videos. It gets out of hand—I miss prep time and I’ve been late to classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of distress. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on the lesson videos. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on my face, especially on lesson videos. One side looks crooked and kind of swollen to me, and once I see it, I can’t get past it. I start thinking the students, parents, and other staff must be staring at that instead of actually listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face. Especially when I see myself on lesson videos, one side looks crooked and sort of swollen to me. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking about it, and I feel like students or parents or other staff are staring at that instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t really choose to think about it—it just comes in, especially after I see myself on those lesson videos. Once I notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen, I can’t seem to let it go, and I start feeling like everyone must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of these thoughts about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep adjusting the camera angle and replaying clips over and over. I’ll cover part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better, and sometimes I re-record the whole thing again and again. It takes so long that I miss prep work, and I’ve ended up late for classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something terrible would happen if you didn't check your appearance or adjust the camera angle before recording or joining a conference?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say something terrible exactly, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’ll post something where my face looks really off. So I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face, and sometimes I end up re-recording it over and over. It gets out of hand—I miss prep time and I’ve been late to classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of distress. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on the lesson videos. Once I see it, it’s hard to focus on anything else, and I feel like the students, parents, and staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they saw your face as it is, without you adjusting the camera angle or re-recording?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think they’d notice it right away—the crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And then I feel like they’d be focused on that instead of what I’m saying, like students or parents or staff would just be staring at it and judging me.\n\nDoctor: When you point out the crooked or swollen side of your face to others, like friends or family, do they notice it or agree with your assessment?\n\nPatient: They usually don’t really agree with me, or they’ll say they don’t see what I’m talking about. But I still can’t stop seeing it, especially on the lesson videos. Once I notice that crooked, swollen-looking side, I feel like everyone watching—students, parents, staff—must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and the time you spend adjusting your appearance for videos affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, outside of work I haven’t noticed a clear change with friends or family because of it. It’s mostly tied to the videos and how I look on camera. I suppose I might be more distracted sometimes, but I can’t say it’s affected my relationships in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face are unreasonable or irrational?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might sound unreasonable, because other people don’t seem to see it the way I do. But when I’m looking at the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it feels obvious, like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform these repetitive actions, like adjusting the camera angle or re-recording, to relieve anxiety or discomfort about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like I have to do it, honestly. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I’ll keep changing the camera angle, replaying the clip, sometimes covering part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better. If it still looks wrong, I end up re-recording the whole thing.\n\nIt does calm me down a little for a moment, but then I check again and it starts over. It’s gotten to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes because I’m still trying to get it to look acceptable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on my face, especially on lesson videos. One side looks crooked and kind of swollen to me, and once I see it, I can’t get past it. I start thinking the students, parents, and other staff must be staring at that instead of actually listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face. Especially when I see myself on lesson videos, one side looks crooked and sort of swollen to me. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking about it, and I feel like students or parents or other staff are staring at that instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t really choose to think about it—it just comes in, especially after I see myself on those lesson videos. Once I notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen, I can’t seem to let it go, and I start feeling like everyone must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of these thoughts about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep adjusting the camera angle and replaying clips over and over. I’ll cover part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better, and sometimes I re-record the whole thing again and again. It takes so long that I miss prep work, and I’ve ended up late for classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something terrible would happen if you didn't check your appearance or adjust the camera angle before recording or joining a conference?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say something terrible exactly, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’ll post something where my face looks really off. So I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face, and sometimes I end up re-recording it over and over. It gets out of hand—I miss prep time and I’ve been late to classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of distress. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on the lesson videos. Once I see it, it’s hard to focus on anything else, and I feel like the students, parents, and staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they saw your face as it is, without you adjusting the camera angle or re-recording?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think they’d notice it right away—the crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And then I feel like they’d be focused on that instead of what I’m saying, like students or parents or staff would just be staring at it and judging me.\n\nDoctor: When you point out the crooked or swollen side of your face to others, like friends or family, do they notice it or agree with your assessment?\n\nPatient: They usually don’t really agree with me, or they’ll say they don’t see what I’m talking about. But I still can’t stop seeing it, especially on the lesson videos. Once I notice that crooked, swollen-looking side, I feel like everyone watching—students, parents, staff—must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and the time you spend adjusting your appearance for videos affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, outside of work I haven’t noticed a clear change with friends or family because of it. It’s mostly tied to the videos and how I look on camera. I suppose I might be more distracted sometimes, but I can’t say it’s affected my relationships in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face are unreasonable or irrational?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might sound unreasonable, because other people don’t seem to see it the way I do. But when I’m looking at the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it feels obvious, like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform these repetitive actions, like adjusting the camera angle or re-recording, to relieve anxiety or discomfort about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like I have to do it, honestly. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I’ll keep changing the camera angle, replaying the clip, sometimes covering part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better. If it still looks wrong, I end up re-recording the whole thing.\n\nIt does calm me down a little for a moment, but then I check again and it starts over. It’s gotten to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes because I’m still trying to get it to look acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face if you never had to be on video or be seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… if no one ever saw me, maybe it wouldn’t feel as urgent. But I’d probably still notice it. The main thing is seeing my face on the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side — and then I get stuck thinking everyone watching must be focused on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your concerns about your face are related to how it functions, or is it purely about how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks. I’m not thinking my face doesn’t work right or anything like that. I just can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked and swollen-looking on the lesson videos, and then I feel like everyone watching is staring at it instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I just can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked and swollen-looking on the lesson videos, and then I feel like everyone watching is staring at it instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you believe that your face actually is crooked and swollen, regardless of what others tell you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it I feel like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on my face, especially on lesson videos. One side looks crooked and kind of swollen to me, and once I see it, I can’t get past it. I start thinking the students, parents, and other staff must be staring at that instead of actually listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face. Especially when I see myself on lesson videos, one side looks crooked and sort of swollen to me. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking about it, and I feel like students or parents or other staff are staring at that instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t really choose to think about it—it just comes in, especially after I see myself on those lesson videos. Once I notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen, I can’t seem to let it go, and I start feeling like everyone must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of these thoughts about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep adjusting the camera angle and replaying clips over and over. I’ll cover part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better, and sometimes I re-record the whole thing again and again. It takes so long that I miss prep work, and I’ve ended up late for classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something terrible would happen if you didn't check your appearance or adjust the camera angle before recording or joining a conference?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say something terrible exactly, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’ll post something where my face looks really off. So I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face, and sometimes I end up re-recording it over and over. It gets out of hand—I miss prep time and I’ve been late to classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of distress. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on the lesson videos. Once I see it, it’s hard to focus on anything else, and I feel like the students, parents, and staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they saw your face as it is, without you adjusting the camera angle or re-recording?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think they’d notice it right away—the crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And then I feel like they’d be focused on that instead of what I’m saying, like students or parents or staff would just be staring at it and judging me.\n\nDoctor: When you point out the crooked or swollen side of your face to others, like friends or family, do they notice it or agree with your assessment?\n\nPatient: They usually don’t really agree with me, or they’ll say they don’t see what I’m talking about. But I still can’t stop seeing it, especially on the lesson videos. Once I notice that crooked, swollen-looking side, I feel like everyone watching—students, parents, staff—must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and the time you spend adjusting your appearance for videos affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, outside of work I haven’t noticed a clear change with friends or family because of it. It’s mostly tied to the videos and how I look on camera. I suppose I might be more distracted sometimes, but I can’t say it’s affected my relationships in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face are unreasonable or irrational?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might sound unreasonable, because other people don’t seem to see it the way I do. But when I’m looking at the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it feels obvious, like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform these repetitive actions, like adjusting the camera angle or re-recording, to relieve anxiety or discomfort about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like I have to do it, honestly. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I’ll keep changing the camera angle, replaying the clip, sometimes covering part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better. If it still looks wrong, I end up re-recording the whole thing.\n\nIt does calm me down a little for a moment, but then I check again and it starts over. It’s gotten to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes because I’m still trying to get it to look acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face if you never had to be on video or be seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… if no one ever saw me, maybe it wouldn’t feel as urgent. But I’d probably still notice it. The main thing is seeing my face on the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side — and then I get stuck thinking everyone watching must be focused on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your concerns about your face are related to how it functions, or is it purely about how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks. I’m not thinking my face doesn’t work right or anything like that. I just can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked and swollen-looking on the lesson videos, and then I feel like everyone watching is staring at it instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your face actually is crooked and swollen, regardless of what others tell you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Even when people tell me it’s not noticeable, I still see that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, especially on lesson videos. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, or staff are staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Once I see it, it’s hard to focus on anything else, and I feel like the students, parents, and staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they saw your face as it is, without you adjusting the camera angle or re-recording?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think they’d notice it right away—the crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And then I feel like they’d be focused on that instead of what I’m saying, like students or parents or staff would just be staring at it and judging me.\n\nDoctor: When you point out the crooked or swollen side of your face to others, like friends or family, do they notice it or agree with your assessment?\n\nPatient: They usually don’t really agree with me, or they’ll say they don’t see what I’m talking about. But I still can’t stop seeing it, especially on the lesson videos. Once I notice that crooked, swollen-looking side, I feel like everyone watching—students, parents, staff—must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and the time you spend adjusting your appearance for videos affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, outside of work I haven’t noticed a clear change with friends or family because of it. It’s mostly tied to the videos and how I look on camera. I suppose I might be more distracted sometimes, but I can’t say it’s affected my relationships in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face are unreasonable or irrational?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might sound unreasonable, because other people don’t seem to see it the way I do. But when I’m looking at the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it feels obvious, like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform these repetitive actions, like adjusting the camera angle or re-recording, to relieve anxiety or discomfort about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like I have to do it, honestly. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I’ll keep changing the camera angle, replaying the clip, sometimes covering part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better. If it still looks wrong, I end up re-recording the whole thing.\n\nIt does calm me down a little for a moment, but then I check again and it starts over. It’s gotten to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes because I’m still trying to get it to look acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face if you never had to be on video or be seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… if no one ever saw me, maybe it wouldn’t feel as urgent. But I’d probably still notice it. The main thing is seeing my face on the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side — and then I get stuck thinking everyone watching must be focused on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your concerns about your face are related to how it functions, or is it purely about how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks. I’m not thinking my face doesn’t work right or anything like that. I just can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked and swollen-looking on the lesson videos, and then I feel like everyone watching is staring at it instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your face actually is crooked and swollen, regardless of what others tell you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Even when people tell me it’s not noticeable, I still see that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, especially on lesson videos. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, or staff are staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look crooked and swollen to someone who didn't know you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would. That’s the problem. When I see myself on the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and I feel like anyone watching — even someone who doesn’t know me — would notice it and stare at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on my face, especially on lesson videos. One side looks crooked and kind of swollen to me, and once I see it, I can’t get past it. I start thinking the students, parents, and other staff must be staring at that instead of actually listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face. Especially when I see myself on lesson videos, one side looks crooked and sort of swollen to me. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking about it, and I feel like students or parents or other staff are staring at that instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t really choose to think about it—it just comes in, especially after I see myself on those lesson videos. Once I notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen, I can’t seem to let it go, and I start feeling like everyone must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of these thoughts about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep adjusting the camera angle and replaying clips over and over. I’ll cover part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better, and sometimes I re-record the whole thing again and again. It takes so long that I miss prep work, and I’ve ended up late for classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something terrible would happen if you didn't check your appearance or adjust the camera angle before recording or joining a conference?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say something terrible exactly, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’ll post something where my face looks really off. So I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face, and sometimes I end up re-recording it over and over. It gets out of hand—I miss prep time and I’ve been late to classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of distress. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on the lesson videos. Once I see it, it’s hard to focus on anything else, and I feel like the students, parents, and staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they saw your face as it is, without you adjusting the camera angle or re-recording?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think they’d notice it right away—the crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And then I feel like they’d be focused on that instead of what I’m saying, like students or parents or staff would just be staring at it and judging me.\n\nDoctor: When you point out the crooked or swollen side of your face to others, like friends or family, do they notice it or agree with your assessment?\n\nPatient: They usually don’t really agree with me, or they’ll say they don’t see what I’m talking about. But I still can’t stop seeing it, especially on the lesson videos. Once I notice that crooked, swollen-looking side, I feel like everyone watching—students, parents, staff—must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and the time you spend adjusting your appearance for videos affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, outside of work I haven’t noticed a clear change with friends or family because of it. It’s mostly tied to the videos and how I look on camera. I suppose I might be more distracted sometimes, but I can’t say it’s affected my relationships in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face are unreasonable or irrational?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might sound unreasonable, because other people don’t seem to see it the way I do. But when I’m looking at the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it feels obvious, like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform these repetitive actions, like adjusting the camera angle or re-recording, to relieve anxiety or discomfort about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like I have to do it, honestly. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I’ll keep changing the camera angle, replaying the clip, sometimes covering part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better. If it still looks wrong, I end up re-recording the whole thing.\n\nIt does calm me down a little for a moment, but then I check again and it starts over. It’s gotten to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes because I’m still trying to get it to look acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face if you never had to be on video or be seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… if no one ever saw me, maybe it wouldn’t feel as urgent. But I’d probably still notice it. The main thing is seeing my face on the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side — and then I get stuck thinking everyone watching must be focused on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your concerns about your face are related to how it functions, or is it purely about how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks. I’m not thinking my face doesn’t work right or anything like that. I just can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked and swollen-looking on the lesson videos, and then I feel like everyone watching is staring at it instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your face actually is crooked and swollen, regardless of what others tell you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Even when people tell me it’s not noticeable, I still see that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, especially on lesson videos. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, or staff are staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look crooked and swollen to someone who didn't know you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would. That’s the problem. When I see myself on the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and I feel like anyone watching — even someone who doesn’t know me — would notice it and stare at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is a fact, regardless of what anyone else says?\n\nPatient: Yes… it feels like a fact to me. I know people can say they don’t see it, but when I look at those lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing one side of my face as crooked and swollen-looking. And once I see it, I feel sure the students, parents, or staff must be staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Once I notice that crooked, swollen-looking side, I feel like everyone watching—students, parents, staff—must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and the time you spend adjusting your appearance for videos affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, outside of work I haven’t noticed a clear change with friends or family because of it. It’s mostly tied to the videos and how I look on camera. I suppose I might be more distracted sometimes, but I can’t say it’s affected my relationships in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face are unreasonable or irrational?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might sound unreasonable, because other people don’t seem to see it the way I do. But when I’m looking at the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it feels obvious, like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform these repetitive actions, like adjusting the camera angle or re-recording, to relieve anxiety or discomfort about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like I have to do it, honestly. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I’ll keep changing the camera angle, replaying the clip, sometimes covering part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better. If it still looks wrong, I end up re-recording the whole thing.\n\nIt does calm me down a little for a moment, but then I check again and it starts over. It’s gotten to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes because I’m still trying to get it to look acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face if you never had to be on video or be seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… if no one ever saw me, maybe it wouldn’t feel as urgent. But I’d probably still notice it. The main thing is seeing my face on the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side — and then I get stuck thinking everyone watching must be focused on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your concerns about your face are related to how it functions, or is it purely about how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks. I’m not thinking my face doesn’t work right or anything like that. I just can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked and swollen-looking on the lesson videos, and then I feel like everyone watching is staring at it instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your face actually is crooked and swollen, regardless of what others tell you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Even when people tell me it’s not noticeable, I still see that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, especially on lesson videos. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, or staff are staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look crooked and swollen to someone who didn't know you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would. That’s the problem. When I see myself on the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and I feel like anyone watching — even someone who doesn’t know me — would notice it and stare at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is a fact, regardless of what anyone else says?\n\nPatient: Yes… it feels like a fact to me. I know people can say they don’t see it, but when I look at those lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing one side of my face as crooked and swollen-looking. And once I see it, I feel sure the students, parents, or staff must be staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about your face looking crooked or swollen when you're alone, not being recorded or seen by others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do worry about it when I’m alone too, but it’s much worse when I’m thinking about the lesson videos or being seen. I keep picturing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and then I start thinking students, parents, or staff must notice it and focus on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on my face, especially on lesson videos. One side looks crooked and kind of swollen to me, and once I see it, I can’t get past it. I start thinking the students, parents, and other staff must be staring at that instead of actually listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face. Especially when I see myself on lesson videos, one side looks crooked and sort of swollen to me. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking about it, and I feel like students or parents or other staff are staring at that instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t really choose to think about it—it just comes in, especially after I see myself on those lesson videos. Once I notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen, I can’t seem to let it go, and I start feeling like everyone must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of these thoughts about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep adjusting the camera angle and replaying clips over and over. I’ll cover part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better, and sometimes I re-record the whole thing again and again. It takes so long that I miss prep work, and I’ve ended up late for classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something terrible would happen if you didn't check your appearance or adjust the camera angle before recording or joining a conference?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say something terrible exactly, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’ll post something where my face looks really off. So I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face, and sometimes I end up re-recording it over and over. It gets out of hand—I miss prep time and I’ve been late to classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of distress. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on the lesson videos. Once I see it, it’s hard to focus on anything else, and I feel like the students, parents, and staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they saw your face as it is, without you adjusting the camera angle or re-recording?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think they’d notice it right away—the crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And then I feel like they’d be focused on that instead of what I’m saying, like students or parents or staff would just be staring at it and judging me.\n\nDoctor: When you point out the crooked or swollen side of your face to others, like friends or family, do they notice it or agree with your assessment?\n\nPatient: They usually don’t really agree with me, or they’ll say they don’t see what I’m talking about. But I still can’t stop seeing it, especially on the lesson videos. Once I notice that crooked, swollen-looking side, I feel like everyone watching—students, parents, staff—must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and the time you spend adjusting your appearance for videos affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, outside of work I haven’t noticed a clear change with friends or family because of it. It’s mostly tied to the videos and how I look on camera. I suppose I might be more distracted sometimes, but I can’t say it’s affected my relationships in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face are unreasonable or irrational?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might sound unreasonable, because other people don’t seem to see it the way I do. But when I’m looking at the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it feels obvious, like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform these repetitive actions, like adjusting the camera angle or re-recording, to relieve anxiety or discomfort about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like I have to do it, honestly. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I’ll keep changing the camera angle, replaying the clip, sometimes covering part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better. If it still looks wrong, I end up re-recording the whole thing.\n\nIt does calm me down a little for a moment, but then I check again and it starts over. It’s gotten to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes because I’m still trying to get it to look acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face if you never had to be on video or be seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… if no one ever saw me, maybe it wouldn’t feel as urgent. But I’d probably still notice it. The main thing is seeing my face on the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side — and then I get stuck thinking everyone watching must be focused on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your concerns about your face are related to how it functions, or is it purely about how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks. I’m not thinking my face doesn’t work right or anything like that. I just can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked and swollen-looking on the lesson videos, and then I feel like everyone watching is staring at it instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your face actually is crooked and swollen, regardless of what others tell you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Even when people tell me it’s not noticeable, I still see that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, especially on lesson videos. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, or staff are staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look crooked and swollen to someone who didn't know you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would. That’s the problem. When I see myself on the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and I feel like anyone watching — even someone who doesn’t know me — would notice it and stare at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is a fact, regardless of what anyone else says?\n\nPatient: Yes… it feels like a fact to me. Even when people tell me it’s not noticeable, I still see that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, especially on lesson videos. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, or staff are staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look crooked and swollen to someone who didn't know you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would. That’s the problem. When I see myself on the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and I feel like anyone watching — even someone who doesn’t know me — would notice it and stare at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is a fact, regardless of what anyone else says?\n\nPatient: Yes… it feels like a fact to me. I know people can say they don’t see it, but when I look at those lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing one side of my face as crooked and swollen-looking. And once I see it, I feel sure the students, parents, or staff must be staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about your face looking crooked or swollen when you're alone, not being recorded or seen by others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do worry about it when I’m alone too, but it’s much worse when I’m thinking about the lesson videos or being seen. I keep picturing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and then I start thinking students, parents, or staff must notice it and focus on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check and adjust your appearance even if you were certain that no one else would notice the flaw you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even if I was told no one would notice, I think I’d still need to check. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face with my hand… sometimes I re-record the whole thing over and over. It gets to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on my face, especially on lesson videos. One side looks crooked and kind of swollen to me, and once I see it, I can’t get past it. I start thinking the students, parents, and other staff must be staring at that instead of actually listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face. Especially when I see myself on lesson videos, one side looks crooked and sort of swollen to me. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking about it, and I feel like students or parents or other staff are staring at that instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t really choose to think about it—it just comes in, especially after I see myself on those lesson videos. Once I notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen, I can’t seem to let it go, and I start feeling like everyone must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of these thoughts about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep adjusting the camera angle and replaying clips over and over. I’ll cover part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better, and sometimes I re-record the whole thing again and again. It takes so long that I miss prep work, and I’ve ended up late for classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something terrible would happen if you didn't check your appearance or adjust the camera angle before recording or joining a conference?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say something terrible exactly, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’ll post something where my face looks really off. So I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face, and sometimes I end up re-recording it over and over. It gets out of hand—I miss prep time and I’ve been late to classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of distress. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on the lesson videos. Once I see it, it’s hard to focus on anything else, and I feel like the students, parents, and staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they saw your face as it is, without you adjusting the camera angle or re-recording?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think they’d notice it right away—the crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And then I feel like they’d be focused on that instead of what I’m saying, like students or parents or staff would just be staring at it and judging me.\n\nDoctor: When you point out the crooked or swollen side of your face to others, like friends or family, do they notice it or agree with your assessment?\n\nPatient: They usually don’t really agree with me, or they’ll say they don’t see what I’m talking about. But I still can’t stop seeing it, especially on the lesson videos. Once I notice that crooked, swollen-looking side, I feel like everyone watching—students, parents, staff—must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and the time you spend adjusting your appearance for videos affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, outside of work I haven’t noticed a clear change with friends or family because of it. It’s mostly tied to the videos and how I look on camera. I suppose I might be more distracted sometimes, but I can’t say it’s affected my relationships in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face are unreasonable or irrational?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might sound unreasonable, because other people don’t seem to see it the way I do. But when I’m looking at the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it feels obvious, like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform these repetitive actions, like adjusting the camera angle or re-recording, to relieve anxiety or discomfort about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like I have to do it, honestly. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I’ll keep changing the camera angle, replaying the clip, sometimes covering part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better. If it still looks wrong, I end up re-recording the whole thing.\n\nIt does calm me down a little for a moment, but then I check again and it starts over. It’s gotten to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes because I’m still trying to get it to look acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face if you never had to be on video or be seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… if no one ever saw me, maybe it wouldn’t feel as urgent. But I’d probably still notice it. The main thing is seeing my face on the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side — and then I get stuck thinking everyone watching must be focused on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your concerns about your face are related to how it functions, or is it purely about how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks. I’m not thinking my face doesn’t work right or anything like that. I just can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked and swollen-looking on the lesson videos, and then I feel like everyone watching is staring at it instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your face actually is crooked and swollen, regardless of what others tell you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Even when people tell me it’s not noticeable, I still see that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, especially on lesson videos. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, or staff are staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look crooked and swollen to someone who didn't know you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would. That’s the problem. When I see myself on the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and I feel like anyone watching — even someone who doesn’t know me — would notice it and stare at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is a fact, regardless of what anyone else says?\n\nPatient: Yes… it feels like a fact to me. I know people can say they don’t see it, but when I look at those lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing one side of my face as crooked and swollen-looking. And once I see it, I feel sure the students, parents, or staff must be staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about your face looking crooked or swollen when you're alone, not being recorded or seen by others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do worry about it when I’m alone too, but it’s much worse when I’m thinking about the lesson videos or being seen. I keep picturing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and then I start thinking students, parents, or staff must notice it and focus on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check and adjust your appearance even if you were certain that no one else would notice the flaw you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even if I was told no one would notice, I think I’d still need to check. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face with my hand… sometimes I re-record the whole thing over and over. It gets to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. It’s not only about other people seeing it, even though that makes it worse. I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked and swollen, especially after seeing myself on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, it just stays in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop focusing on my face, especially on lesson videos. One side looks crooked and kind of swollen to me, and once I see it, I can’t get past it. I start thinking the students, parents, and other staff must be staring at that instead of actually listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face. Especially when I see myself on lesson videos, one side looks crooked and sort of swollen to me. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking about it, and I feel like students or parents or other staff are staring at that instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t really choose to think about it—it just comes in, especially after I see myself on those lesson videos. Once I notice that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen, I can’t seem to let it go, and I start feeling like everyone must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors or touching your face, because of these thoughts about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep adjusting the camera angle and replaying clips over and over. I’ll cover part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better, and sometimes I re-record the whole thing again and again. It takes so long that I miss prep work, and I’ve ended up late for classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something terrible would happen if you didn't check your appearance or adjust the camera angle before recording or joining a conference?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say something terrible exactly, but it feels like if I don’t check, I’ll post something where my face looks really off. So I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face, and sometimes I end up re-recording it over and over. It gets out of hand—I miss prep time and I’ve been late to classes because of it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts about your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It causes a lot of distress. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked or swollen on the lesson videos. Once I see it, it’s hard to focus on anything else, and I feel like the students, parents, and staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would judge you or think less of you if they saw your face as it is, without you adjusting the camera angle or re-recording?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think they’d notice it right away—the crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And then I feel like they’d be focused on that instead of what I’m saying, like students or parents or staff would just be staring at it and judging me.\n\nDoctor: When you point out the crooked or swollen side of your face to others, like friends or family, do they notice it or agree with your assessment?\n\nPatient: They usually don’t really agree with me, or they’ll say they don’t see what I’m talking about. But I still can’t stop seeing it, especially on the lesson videos. Once I notice that crooked, swollen-looking side, I feel like everyone watching—students, parents, staff—must be staring at it instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and the time you spend adjusting your appearance for videos affected your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, outside of work I haven’t noticed a clear change with friends or family because of it. It’s mostly tied to the videos and how I look on camera. I suppose I might be more distracted sometimes, but I can’t say it’s affected my relationships in a definite way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face are unreasonable or irrational?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might sound unreasonable, because other people don’t seem to see it the way I do. But when I’m looking at the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. Once I notice it, it feels obvious, like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform these repetitive actions, like adjusting the camera angle or re-recording, to relieve anxiety or discomfort about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like I have to do it, honestly. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I’ll keep changing the camera angle, replaying the clip, sometimes covering part of my face with my hand to see if it looks better. If it still looks wrong, I end up re-recording the whole thing.\n\nIt does calm me down a little for a moment, but then I check again and it starts over. It’s gotten to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes because I’m still trying to get it to look acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face if you never had to be on video or be seen by others?\n\nPatient: I think… if no one ever saw me, maybe it wouldn’t feel as urgent. But I’d probably still notice it. The main thing is seeing my face on the lesson videos — that crooked, swollen-looking side — and then I get stuck thinking everyone watching must be focused on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your concerns about your face are related to how it functions, or is it purely about how it looks?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks. I’m not thinking my face doesn’t work right or anything like that. I just can’t stop seeing that one side as crooked and swollen-looking on the lesson videos, and then I feel like everyone watching is staring at it instead of paying attention to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your face actually is crooked and swollen, regardless of what others tell you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Even when people tell me it’s not noticeable, I still see that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, especially on lesson videos. Once I notice it, I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, or staff are staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look crooked and swollen to someone who didn't know you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would. That’s the problem. When I see myself on the lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and I feel like anyone watching — even someone who doesn’t know me — would notice it and stare at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face being crooked and swollen is a fact, regardless of what anyone else says?\n\nPatient: Yes… it feels like a fact to me. I know people can say they don’t see it, but when I look at those lesson videos, I can’t stop seeing one side of my face as crooked and swollen-looking. And once I see it, I feel sure the students, parents, or staff must be staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about your face looking crooked or swollen when you're alone, not being recorded or seen by others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do worry about it when I’m alone too, but it’s much worse when I’m thinking about the lesson videos or being seen. I keep picturing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and then I start thinking students, parents, or staff must notice it and focus on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check and adjust your appearance even if you were certain that no one else would notice the flaw you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even if I was told no one would notice, I think I’d still need to check. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face with my hand… sometimes I re-record the whole thing over and over. It gets to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. It’s not only about other people seeing it, even though that makes it worse. I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked and swollen, especially after seeing myself on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, it just stays in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about your face would decrease if you were certain that others did not judge you based on your appearance?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would go away. The main thing is I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I start feeling like the students, parents, or staff must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And once I see it, I feel sure the students, parents, or staff must be staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about your face looking crooked or swollen when you're alone, not being recorded or seen by others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do worry about it when I’m alone too, but it’s much worse when I’m thinking about the lesson videos or being seen. I keep picturing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face, and then I start thinking students, parents, or staff must notice it and focus on that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still feel the need to check and adjust your appearance even if you were certain that no one else would notice the flaw you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even if I was told no one would notice, I think I’d still need to check. Before I post a recorded lesson or join a parent conference, I keep changing the camera angle, replaying clips, covering part of my face with my hand… sometimes I re-record the whole thing over and over. It gets to the point where I miss prep work or end up late for classes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. It’s not only about other people seeing it, even though that makes it worse. I can’t stop noticing that one side of my face looks crooked and swollen, especially after seeing myself on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, it just stays in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about your face would decrease if you were certain that others did not judge you based on your appearance?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would go away. The main thing is I can’t stop seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I start feeling like the students, parents, or staff must be staring at it instead of listening to what I’m saying."
